The approach could be extended to more complex MHD models and used for parameter exploration, uncertainty quantification, or control. The modular approach and combination of operator learning with stochastic models can inspire similar approaches to other physically challenging problems.

**Score:** 8

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and significant advance in surrogate modeling for MHD turbulence. The hybrid PINO-diffusion framework demonstrates clear improvements over existing PINO-only approaches, particularly in high Reynolds number regimes. While the training cost and generality need to be further investigated, the strong results and well-reasoned methodology warrant a high score. The potential impact on the field is considerable, offering a pathway toward more efficient and accurate MHD simulations. The weaknesses noted above are mostly concerned with future extensibility, and the immediate technical achievement is significant enough to merit the assigned score.

- **Classification**: physics.flu-dyn
- **Score**: 8/10

### Scaling Collapse Reveals Universal Dynamics in Compute-Optimally Trained Neural Networks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02119v1)
- **Authors**: Shikai Qiu, Lechao Xiao, Andrew Gordon Wilson, Jeffrey Pennington, Atish Agarwala
- **Abstract**: What scaling limits govern neural network training dynamics when model size and training time grow in tandem? We show that despite the complex interactions between architecture, training algorithms, and data, compute-optimally trained models exhibit a remarkably precise universality. Specifically, loss curves from models of varying sizes collapse onto a single universal curve when training compute and loss are normalized to unity at the end of training. With learning rate decay, the collapse becomes so tight that differences in the normalized curves across models fall below the noise floor of individual loss curves across random seeds, a phenomenon we term supercollapse. We observe supercollapse across learning rate schedules, datasets, and architectures, including transformers trained on next-token prediction, and find it breaks down when hyperparameters are scaled suboptimally, providing a precise and practical indicator of good scaling. We explain these phenomena by connecting collapse to the power-law structure in typical neural scaling laws, and analyzing a simple yet surprisingly effective model of SGD noise dynamics that accurately predicts loss curves across various learning rate schedules and quantitatively explains the origin of supercollapse.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Scaling Collapse Reveals Universal Dynamics in Compute-Optimally Trained Neural Networks" investigates the training dynamics of neural networks as model size and training compute scale together. The key finding is the phenomenon of "scaling collapse" – where the loss curves of compute-optimally trained models of varying sizes collapse onto a single universal curve after normalization. This effect is significantly amplified with learning rate decay, leading to "supercollapse," where cross-scale differences fall below the noise floor of individual loss curves. The authors observe this across various architectures, datasets, and learning rate schedules. They connect this collapse to power-law scaling and develop a simple model of SGD noise dynamics to predict loss curves and explain supercollapse quantitatively. Deviations from collapse serve as a practical indicator of suboptimal scaling choices.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates a surprisingly precise universality in the training dynamics of compute-optimally trained neural networks. While the existence of neural scaling laws and some predictability in training dynamics was known, the degree of precise collapse onto a *single* universal curve, especially with learning rate decay (supercollapse), is a novel finding. The distinction between the observed collapse and the roughly power-law behavior described in Kaplan et al. (2020) strengthens the claim. The concept of "supercollapse" as a scaling diagnostic is also novel and practical. The specific model of SGD noise connecting learning rate to collapse quality is another novel aspect of the paper.
*   **Significance:** The findings have several significant implications:

    *   **Improved Understanding of Scaling:** It deepens our understanding of what governs the training process as model size and training time grow in tandem. It suggests an underlying simplicity even in complex training scenarios.
    *   **Practical Scaling Diagnostic:**  Supercollapse serves as a powerful tool for identifying and correcting misconfigurations in scaling choices, potentially saving significant computational resources in large-scale training. The paper demonstrates this in some detail with regards to suboptimal data scaling and model parameterization.
    *   **Theoretical Implications:** The observed joint scaling limit, where model size and training time grow together while maintaining consistency, challenges traditional infinite-width/depth limits, which often diverge as training progresses.
    *   **Predictive Power:** The simple noise model accurately predicts loss curves across various learning rate schedules, offering potential for optimizing training.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Empirical Evidence:** The paper provides strong empirical support for its claims across various datasets (CIFAR-5M, Lichess) and architectures (Transformers, MLPs).
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and structured, making the key concepts accessible. The figures effectively illustrate the main findings.
    *   **Theoretical Grounding:** The theoretical analysis, while simple, provides a plausible explanation for the observed phenomena, linking the collapse to power-law scaling and noise dynamics.
    *   **Code Availability:** Makes the work reproducible.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Simplicity of Theoretical Model:** While effective, the theoretical model is relatively simple. More sophisticated models could potentially provide a deeper understanding of the underlying mechanisms.
    *   **Limited Scale of Experiments:**  The experiments, while sufficient to demonstrate the principles, are conducted on relatively smaller models and datasets. Validation on significantly larger models and more complex, real-world datasets would further strengthen the conclusions. While the paper claims generality across diverse experimental setups, the tasks are still limited, with no apparent reinforcement learning or image generation applications.
    *   **Reliance on Compute-Optimal Training:** The focus on compute-optimal training, while practically relevant, might limit the generalizability of the findings to scenarios where resources are not optimally allocated. It's an open question how robust the collapse is to deviations from this regime.

*   **Justification of Score:** The paper presents a novel and significant finding with strong empirical support and a plausible theoretical explanation. The practical implications for scaling diagnostics and the theoretical challenges it poses warrant a high score. While the simplicity of the theoretical model and limited scale of experiments are limitations, the core findings are compelling and have the potential to influence future research in neural network scaling.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### PAL: Designing Conversational Agents as Scalable, Cooperative Patient Simulators for Palliative-Care Training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02122v1)
- **Authors**: Neil K. R. Sehgal, Hita Kambhamettu, Allen Chang, Andrew Zhu, Lyle Ungar, Sharath Chandra Guntuku
- **Abstract**: Effective communication in serious illness and palliative care is essential but often under-taught due to limited access to training resources like standardized patients. We present PAL (Palliative Assisted Learning-bot), a conversational system that simulates emotionally nuanced patient interactions and delivers structured feedback grounded in an existing empathy-based framework. PAL supports text and voice modalities and is designed to scaffold clinical skill-building through repeated, low-cost practice. Through a mixed-methods study with 17 U.S. medical trainees and clinicians, we explore user engagement with PAL, evaluate usability, and examine design tensions around modalities, emotional realism, and feedback delivery. Participants found PAL helpful for reflection and skill refinement, though some noted limitations in emotional authenticity and the adaptability of feedback. We contribute: (1) empirical evidence that large language models can support palliative communication training; (2) design insights for modality-aware, emotionally sensitive simulation tools; and (3) implications for systems that support emotional labor, cooperative learning, and AI-augmented training in high-stakes care settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PAL (Palliative Assisted Learning-bot), a web-based chatbot designed to simulate emotionally nuanced patient interactions for palliative care communication training. PAL utilizes large language models (LLMs) to generate context-sensitive patient responses and provides structured feedback grounded in the NURSE empathy framework. The system supports both text and voice modalities, allowing trainees to practice difficult conversations on demand. A mixed-methods study with 17 U.S. medical trainees and clinicians evaluated PAL's usability, modality design, and feedback mechanisms. The study found that PAL facilitated skill-building, though some participants noted limitations in emotional authenticity and the adaptability of feedback. The authors contribute empirical evidence for LLM-driven simulation in palliative care, design insights for modality-aware simulation tools, and implications for systems that support emotional labor and clinical collaboration in high-stakes care settings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates a novel application of LLMs in a sensitive and complex domain: palliative care communication. While LLM-driven simulations exist for general medical training, PAL specifically targets the emotional nuances of end-of-life conversations, which are often overlooked. The integration of the NURSE framework to provide structured, empathy-focused feedback is also a valuable contribution. The incorporation of voice and text modalities and the insights around their relative strengths and weaknesses adds to the novelty.
*   **Significance:**  Effective communication in palliative care is critical for patient well-being and satisfaction, yet often under-taught. PAL addresses a significant gap by providing a scalable and low-cost training solution that complements traditional methods like standardized patients. The system can potentially improve the quality of care delivered by clinicians who may lack sufficient training in emotional communication. The study findings provide valuable design insights for future simulation tools in emotionally sensitive domains. Moreover, the paper contributes to a broader understanding of how AI can augment human expertise and support emotional labor in clinical settings.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Real-World Applicability:** Targets a vital, under-resourced area of medical training.
    *   **Mixed-Methods Approach:** Combines quantitative usability ratings with qualitative feedback from participants, providing a comprehensive evaluation.
    *   **Actionable Design Insights:** Identifies specific design tensions and offers recommendations for improvement, such as increasing emotional authenticity and diversifying training scenarios.
    *   **Clear Explanation of Framework:** Effectively details NURSE feedback and its integration into the chatbot's functionality.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Sample Size:** The study's sample size of 17 participants is relatively small, which may limit the generalizability of the findings. A larger, more diverse sample would strengthen the conclusions.
    *   **Lack of Long-Term Assessment:** The study does not assess the long-term impact of PAL on clinical practice. Future research should investigate whether PAL leads to improved communication skills and patient outcomes over time.
    *   **Emotional Authenticity:** While the system is deemed helpful, there are concerns that PAL may be limited in emotional expression, which could benefit from further model tuning.
    *   **NURSE framework Limitations:** Some considered the feedback framework limiting, suggesting future AI-driven systems may improve with a more flexible, nuanced understanding of communication styles.
*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the design and implementation of AI-driven simulation tools in healthcare. It highlights the importance of emotional sensitivity, structured feedback, and modality design in creating effective training experiences. The findings can also inform the development of systems that support emotional labor and clinical collaboration in other high-stakes domains.

**Overall:**
The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of healthcare simulation and AI-assisted training. While the study has some limitations, it offers promising evidence for the use of LLMs in palliative care communication training. The design insights and recommendations for improvement provide a solid foundation for future research and development in this area.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** This score reflects the paper's novelty in applying LLMs to a specific and sensitive area of medical training, its significance in addressing a critical gap in communication skills development, and its potential impact on the design of future simulation tools. The limitations of the study, particularly the small sample size and lack of long-term assessment, prevent a higher score. Nevertheless, the paper offers a solid foundation for future research and contributes valuable insights to the field.

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Generative Latent Diffusion for Efficient Spatiotemporal Data Reduction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02129v1)
- **Authors**: Xiao Li, Liangji Zhu, Anand Rangarajan, Sanjay Ranka
- **Abstract**: Generative models have demonstrated strong performance in conditional settings and can be viewed as a form of data compression, where the condition serves as a compact representation. However, their limited controllability and reconstruction accuracy restrict their practical application to data compression. In this work, we propose an efficient latent diffusion framework that bridges this gap by combining a variational autoencoder with a conditional diffusion model. Our method compresses only a small number of keyframes into latent space and uses them as conditioning inputs to reconstruct the remaining frames via generative interpolation, eliminating the need to store latent representations for every frame. This approach enables accurate spatiotemporal reconstruction while significantly reducing storage costs. Experimental results across multiple datasets show that our method achieves up to 10 times higher compression ratios than rule-based state-of-the-art compressors such as SZ3, and up to 63 percent better performance than leading learning-based methods under the same reconstruction error.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces a novel generative data compression framework that leverages latent diffusion models for efficient spatiotemporal data reduction in scientific applications. The core idea is to compress only a subset of frames (keyframes) into a compact latent space using a variational autoencoder (VAE) with a hyperprior. The remaining frames are reconstructed through a conditional diffusion model, which interpolates between the keyframes based on their latent representations.  A key aspect is the inclusion of a post-processing step to guarantee error bounds on the reconstructed data, essential for maintaining data integrity in scientific workflows. The method is evaluated across diverse scientific datasets (E3SM, S3D, JHTDB) and compared to existing rule-based (SZ3, ZFP) and learning-based (CDC, GCD, VAE-SR) compression techniques. The results demonstrate significant improvements in compression ratio, achieving up to 10x higher compression than rule-based methods and up to 63% better than learning-based approaches while maintaining comparable reconstruction error.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using latent diffusion models for *interpolation* within a compression framework, rather than direct synthesis, is a definite strength.  Prior diffusion-based compression works required storing a latent representation for every frame/block, limiting their efficiency. Selectively storing keyframes and using diffusion for reconstruction is a novel contribution. The integration with a VAE and hyperprior is reasonably standard, but the overall architecture shows some clear ingenuity. The post-processing step to guarantee error bounds is another good choice. The authors combine a few different techniques but integrate them in an original way.

*   **Significance:** High-resolution spatiotemporal datasets are prevalent in scientific computing, making efficient compression critical. The method's ability to achieve significant compression improvements while guaranteeing error bounds positions it as a valuable tool for various scientific domains. The experimental results are solid and comprehensive, covering multiple datasets and comparing against state-of-the-art methods. However, a crucial concern is whether the method is truly *practical*. Although it can be more efficient, the generative nature of diffusion models is still resource-intensive compared to rule-based compressors, which could limit adoption in some use cases. Furthermore, the approach requires careful tuning of keyframe intervals and the number of denoising steps which can limit its application. The results indicate domain-specific intervals yield the best performance which is good for customized application but limits ease-of-use.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Demonstrated significant improvement in compression ratio over existing methods.
    *   Incorporates error-bound guarantees, a must-have for scientific applications.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation on diverse datasets.
    *   Novel integration of latent diffusion models for efficient interpolation.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Complexity and computational overhead of diffusion models compared to rule-based methods might hinder widespread adoption. Generative AI models are computationally expensive in comparison to standard compressors.
    *   Requires careful hyperparameter tuning (keyframe interval, denoising steps) that might need to be redone per application.
    *   The post-processing step uses CPU, which causes a bottleneck, indicating there is a computational cost associated with guarantees.
    *   Lack of any study of the effects the method has on analysis and quantities of interest. The authors only examine the reconstruction error, ignoring if downstream tasks are impacted.

*   **Potential Impact:** If the performance improvements translate to real-world scientific workflows, this method has the potential to significantly reduce storage costs and enable more complex simulations.  The integration of error-bound guarantees makes it attractive for applications where data fidelity is paramount. The novelty of using interpolation to avoid memory issues will likely spawn further research.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**  The paper presents a well-executed approach with clear novelty in its use of diffusion models for interpolation in a compression framework. The results are significant, demonstrating considerable improvements over existing methods. However, the method's complexity, computational demands, domain-specific keyframes, and CPU bottleneck are considerable limitations that prevent it from achieving a higher score. While the contribution is solid, it also has its drawbacks in both computational burden and ease-of-use. This puts it in the range of a meaningful advance.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Dissecting the Impact of Mobile DVFS Governors on LLM Inference Performance and Energy Efficiency
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02135v1)
- **Authors**: Zongpu Zhang, Pranab Dash, Y. Charlie Hu, Qiang Xu, Jian Li, Haibing Guan
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly being integrated into various applications and services running on billions of mobile devices. However, deploying LLMs on resource-limited mobile devices faces a significant challenge due to their high demand for computation, memory, and ultimately energy. While current LLM frameworks for mobile use three power-hungry components-CPU, GPU, and Memory-even when running primarily-GPU LLM models, optimized DVFS governors for CPU, GPU, and memory featured in modern mobile devices operate independently and are oblivious of each other. Motivated by the above observation, in this work, we first measure the energy-efficiency of a SOTA LLM framework consisting of various LLM models on mobile phones which showed the triplet mobile governors result in up to 40.4% longer prefilling and decoding latency compared to optimal combinations of CPU, GPU, and memory frequencies with the same energy consumption for sampled prefill and decode lengths. Second, we conduct an in-depth measurement study to uncover how the intricate interplay (or lack of) among the mobile governors cause the above inefficiency in LLM inference. Finally, based on these insights, we design FUSE - a unified energy-aware governor for optimizing the energy efficiency of LLM inference on mobile devices. Our evaluation using a ShareGPT dataset shows FUSE reduces the time-to-first-token and time-per-output-token latencies by 7.0%-16.9% and 25.4%-36.8% on average with the same energy-per-token for various mobile LLM models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

This paper investigates the impact of Dynamic Voltage and Frequency Scaling (DVFS) governors on the performance and energy efficiency of Large Language Model (LLM) inference on mobile devices. It reveals that existing mobile DVFS governors, which independently manage CPU, GPU, and memory frequencies, lead to suboptimal LLM inference due to antagonistic interactions (i.e., a "downward spiral" where governors drive each other to lower frequencies). The authors propose FUSE, a unified energy-aware governor that profiles optimal frequency combinations offline and applies them at runtime.  Experimental results demonstrate that FUSE reduces inference latency with comparable energy consumption compared to the default governors.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in identifying and characterizing the interference between independent DVFS governors on mobile devices when running LLMs. While DVFS optimization and LLM inference are individually well-studied, the examination of their interaction on resource-constrained mobile platforms is a significant contribution. The proposed FUSE governor, while building upon existing DVFS techniques, offers a practical solution tailored to LLM inference, which enhances the novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial challenge of deploying LLMs on mobile devices: energy efficiency. LLMs have widespread application and enabling efficient inference on mobile platforms improves user experience and broadens accessibility of LLM technology. The demonstrated improvements in latency and energy efficiency using FUSE highlight the practical significance of the work.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Thorough Experimental Analysis:** The paper presents a well-designed and detailed experimental methodology. The controlled experiments isolating the impact of individual governors and identifying antagonistic interactions offer strong evidence. The use of real mobile devices and popular LLM frameworks strengthens the validity and practical relevance of the findings.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition and Solution:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of governor interference and proposes a pragmatic solution (FUSE) that directly addresses it.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation of FUSE:** FUSE is thoroughly evaluated across various LLM models and usage scenarios. The results demonstrate its effectiveness in improving both latency and energy efficiency, validating the approach.
    *   **Public Release of Implementation:** The authors' decision to release FUSE as an extension to `llama.cpp` significantly enhances the impact of their work by enabling further research and adoption.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Governor Scope:** While the investigation is comprehensive for the specific Android phones tested, the findings might not generalize perfectly to all mobile devices with different governor implementations.
    *   **Offline Profiling Overhead:** The offline profiling stage, although effective, adds an initial overhead. The paper could benefit from a discussion of the scalability and adaptability of this profiling stage, especially for dynamically changing workloads. A possible refinement could involve adaptive or semi-online techniques for tuning the DVFS governor.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to significantly impact the field of mobile LLM deployment. By providing a better understanding of DVFS governor interactions, it paves the way for more efficient LLM inference on mobile devices. The open-source release of FUSE promotes further research and development in this direction. The methodology and analysis in the paper also serve as a template for studying similar cross-layer interactions in other resource-constrained environments.

*   **Justification for the Score:** The paper tackles an important problem with a robust methodology and a practical solution. The experiments are thorough, and the findings are clearly presented. While some limitations exist regarding governor scope and offline profiling, the overall contribution is significant and valuable to the mobile LLM community.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.OS
- **Score**: 7/10

### When LLMs Disagree: Diagnosing Relevance Filtering Bias and Retrieval Divergence in SDG Search
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02139v1)
- **Authors**: William A. Ingram, Bipasha Banerjee, Edward A. Fox
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to assign document relevance labels in information retrieval pipelines, especially in domains lacking human-labeled data. However, different models often disagree on borderline cases, raising concerns about how such disagreement affects downstream retrieval. This study examines labeling disagreement between two open-weight LLMs, LLaMA and Qwen, on a corpus of scholarly abstracts related to Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) 1, 3, and 7. We isolate disagreement subsets and examine their lexical properties, rank-order behavior, and classification predictability. Our results show that model disagreement is systematic, not random: disagreement cases exhibit consistent lexical patterns, produce divergent top-ranked outputs under shared scoring functions, and are distinguishable with AUCs above 0.74 using simple classifiers. These findings suggest that LLM-based filtering introduces structured variability in document retrieval, even under controlled prompting and shared ranking logic. We propose using classification disagreement as an object of analysis in retrieval evaluation, particularly in policy-relevant or thematic search tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the impact of disagreement between two large language models (LLMs), LLaMA and Qwen, when used for relevance filtering in the context of Sustainable Development Goal (SDG) document retrieval. It focuses on how this disagreement affects downstream retrieval performance. The authors analyze lexical properties of disagreement cases, assess their predictability, and examine retrieval divergence by measuring differences in top-ranked document sets.  They find that disagreement is systematic, not random, and reflects model-specific preferences, ultimately leading to divergent retrieval profiles even under controlled prompting and shared ranking logic. The paper proposes using classification disagreement as an analytical tool in retrieval evaluation, particularly in policy-relevant or thematic search tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its focus on *diagnosing* and *understanding* LLM disagreement in relevance filtering, rather than simply treating it as noise to be averaged out. While prior work has investigated LLM reliability and prompt sensitivity, this paper specifically examines the downstream retrieval implications of model divergence, particularly in a socially relevant domain like SDG research. The idea of using disagreement as a *diagnostic signal* to reveal hidden biases and filtering preferences is a valuable contribution. The experimental design is reasonable, addressing focused research questions.
* **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical concern in the growing use of LLMs for information retrieval, especially in domains with limited labeled data. The finding that LLM disagreement can lead to divergent retrieval outcomes, even with controlled prompts and shared ranking, has significant implications for researchers and practitioners using LLMs for document filtering and analysis. Understanding these biases is crucial for ensuring fairness, transparency, and robustness in retrieval systems, particularly in policy-related fields. Highlighting potential instability in SDG-aligned retrieval emphasizes the real-world consequences of these biases.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Research Questions:** The paper has well-defined research questions that are addressed systematically.
    * **Rigorous Methodology:** The experimental methodology is clearly described and appropriately applied, including controlled LLM prompting, lexical analysis using TF-IDF, permutation testing, and simulated retrieval experiments.
    * **Relevant Application Domain:** The choice of SDG retrieval is significant because it addresses a socially relevant problem and highlights the potential impact of LLM biases on policy-related search.
    * **Actionable Insights:** The paper provides actionable insights for retrieval evaluation, suggesting the use of disagreement analysis as a tool for understanding and mitigating biases in LLM-based systems.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Model Scope:** The study only considers two LLMs (LLaMA and Qwen) and focuses on three specific SDGs. Generalizability is potentially limited. Examining a more diverse set of models and topics would strengthen the findings.
    * **Surface-Level Analysis:** While the lexical analysis provides valuable insights, the paper could benefit from a deeper semantic analysis of the disagreement cases, perhaps using contextualized embeddings or topic modeling.
    * **Lack of Human Ground Truth:** The absence of a human-labeled ground truth makes it difficult to assess the "correctness" of the LLM relevance judgments, limiting the ability to evaluate the impact of disagreement on retrieval accuracy. The paper adequately acknowledges this limitation by reframing the analysis around interpretive variation rather than correctness.
    * **Limited Retrieval Evaluation:** The retrieval simulation uses centroid-based and representative-query approaches.  A more comprehensive retrieval evaluation using a wider range of queries and evaluation metrics would provide a more robust assessment of the impact of LLM disagreement on retrieval performance.

* **Impact and Influence:**  The paper is likely to influence research in LLM-based information retrieval by highlighting the importance of considering model disagreement and by providing a framework for analyzing and mitigating its effects. It will encourage researchers to move beyond a simplistic view of LLM judgments as proxies for ground truth and to explore the nuances of model behavior under uncertainty.

**Justification:**
The paper deserves a score of 7. The paper makes a valuable contribution by addressing a key issue in LLM-based information retrieval and proposing a novel analytical approach, highlighting the need for careful evaluation and mitigation of biases in LLM-based systems. However, limitations in model scope, depth of analysis, and retrieval evaluation prevent it from reaching a higher score. Nonetheless, the paper's actionable insights and practical implications make it a significant contribution to the field.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Reasoning or Not? A Comprehensive Evaluation of Reasoning LLMs for Dialogue Summarization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02145v1)
- **Authors**: Keyan Jin, Yapeng Wang, Leonel Santos, Tao Fang, Xu Yang, Sio Kei Im, Hugo Gonçalo Oliveira
- **Abstract**: Dialogue summarization is a challenging task with significant practical value in customer service, meeting analysis, and conversational AI. Although large language models (LLMs) have achieved substantial progress in summarization tasks, the performance of step-by-step reasoning architectures-specifically Long Chain-of-Thought (CoT) implementations such as OpenAI-o1 and DeepSeek-R1-remains unexplored for dialogue scenarios requiring concurrent abstraction and conciseness. In this work, we present the first comprehensive and systematic evaluation of state-of-the-art reasoning LLMs and non-reasoning LLMs across three major paradigms-generic, role-oriented, and query-oriented dialogue summarization. Our study spans diverse languages, domains, and summary lengths, leveraging strong benchmarks (SAMSum, DialogSum, CSDS, and QMSum) and advanced evaluation protocols that include both LLM-based automatic metrics and human-inspired criteria. Contrary to trends in other reasoning-intensive tasks, our findings show that explicit stepwise reasoning does not consistently improve dialogue summarization quality. Instead, reasoning LLMs are often prone to verbosity, factual inconsistencies, and less concise summaries compared to their non-reasoning counterparts. Through scenario-specific analyses and detailed case studies, we further identify when and why explicit reasoning may fail to benefit-or even hinder-summarization in complex dialogue contexts. Our work provides new insights into the limitations of current reasoning LLMs and highlights the need for targeted modeling and evaluation strategies for real-world dialogue summarization.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Reasoning or Not? A Comprehensive Evaluation of Reasoning LLMs for Dialogue Summarization" presents a systematic evaluation of reasoning-oriented Large Language Models (LLMs) compared to their non-reasoning counterparts in dialogue summarization tasks. The authors conduct experiments across three main dialogue summarization paradigms (generic, role-oriented, and query-oriented) using diverse datasets, languages, and summary lengths.  They employ both automatic metrics and LLM-based evaluation to assess the quality of the generated summaries. The key finding is that explicit stepwise reasoning, as implemented in current LLMs, does *not* consistently improve dialogue summarization quality and can even be detrimental.  Reasoning LLMs often exhibit verbosity, factual inconsistencies, and less concise summaries. The paper identifies scenarios where reasoning fails to benefit summarization and offers insights into the limitations of current reasoning LLMs for this task.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its comprehensive and systematic evaluation of reasoning LLMs *specifically in the context of dialogue summarization*. While reasoning LLMs have been evaluated in other NLP tasks, this work specifically addresses a gap in understanding their efficacy for dialogue summarization, considering different summarization types, languages, and domains. It's surprising that reasoning LLMs don't consistently outperform base models, a result that is presented in the paper. This challenges the assumption that increased reasoning capabilities automatically translate to better performance in all NLP tasks.  The breakdown into generic, role-oriented, and query-oriented summarization paradigms contributes to a more granular understanding.

* **Significance:** The paper's findings have significant implications for the development and deployment of LLMs for dialogue summarization in real-world applications. It highlights that relying solely on models with enhanced reasoning capabilities might not be optimal and that simpler models can sometimes perform better. The identification of verbosity, factual inconsistencies, and lack of conciseness in reasoning LLM summaries is crucial for guiding future research towards more targeted modeling strategies. The analysis of *why* reasoning fails in certain cases (e.g., excessive elaboration, failure to synthesize information effectively) is valuable for model developers. It also offers important insights into the shortcomings of LLM-based evaluation, pointing towards areas that need improvement. The paper's systematic approach and detailed analysis could lead to more effective and efficient dialogue summarization systems.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The extensive evaluation across multiple datasets, languages, summarization paradigms, and evaluation metrics is a major strength.
    * **Rigorous Methodology:**  The use of standardized prompts and consistent experimental settings ensures fair comparisons.
    * **Detailed Analysis:**  The in-depth analysis of intrinsic summary qualities, the use of LLM-as-a-judge, the examination of reasoning processes, and the case study provide a rich understanding of the models' behavior.
    * **Practical Implications:**  The paper provides practical insights for selecting and developing models tailored to dialogue summarization needs.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Model Selection**: While the authors chose some well-known reasoning and base LLMs, the NLP landscape is rapidly evolving. Including a wider range of models or focusing on the most recent iterations *could* have strengthened the analysis (though it would also significantly increase the scope of the work).
    * **Limited Exploration of Mitigation Strategies:** The paper primarily focuses on identifying the problem, but it could have explored potential mitigation strategies in more detail (e.g., different prompting techniques, fine-tuning methods specifically designed to address the identified issues). Although this might have been outside the scope.
    * **Reliance on Automated Evaluation**:  While the use of LLM-as-a-judge is valuable, it's not a perfect substitute for human evaluation. A small-scale human evaluation of a subset of the results would further validate the findings.

* **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence research directions in several ways. It will encourage researchers to:
    * Re-evaluate the assumptions around reasoning LLMs and their applicability to all NLP tasks.
    * Develop more targeted modeling strategies for dialogue summarization that specifically address the identified limitations of current reasoning LLMs.
    * Improve evaluation methodologies to better capture the nuances of summary quality (e.g., factuality, conciseness, pragmatic adequacy).
    * Explore alternative approaches to incorporating reasoning into dialogue summarization (e.g., different architectures, training methods).

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **7.5**.  The paper presents a novel and significant contribution by rigorously evaluating reasoning LLMs in the under-explored context of dialogue summarization. The surprising findings, the comprehensive methodology, and the detailed analysis make it a valuable contribution to the field. However, the limited exploration of mitigation strategies and the reliance primarily on automated evaluation slightly reduce its impact. The landscape of LLMs also continues to rapidly evolve. Despite these weaknesses, the paper is well-written, clearly presents its findings, and offers important insights that can guide future research.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Generating Large Semi-Synthetic Graphs of Any Size
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02166v1)
- **Authors**: Rodrigo Tuna, Carlos Soares
- **Abstract**: Graph generation is an important area in network science. Traditional approaches focus on replicating specific properties of real-world graphs, such as small diameters or power-law degree distributions. Recent advancements in deep learning, particularly with Graph Neural Networks, have enabled data-driven methods to learn and generate graphs without relying on predefined structural properties. Despite these advances, current models are limited by their reliance on node IDs, which restricts their ability to generate graphs larger than the input graph and ignores node attributes. To address these challenges, we propose Latent Graph Sampling Generation (LGSG), a novel framework that leverages diffusion models and node embeddings to generate graphs of varying sizes without retraining. The framework eliminates the dependency on node IDs and captures the distribution of node embeddings and subgraph structures, enabling scalable and flexible graph generation. Experimental results show that LGSG performs on par with baseline models for standard metrics while outperforming them in overlooked ones, such as the tendency of nodes to form clusters. Additionally, it maintains consistent structural characteristics across graphs of different sizes, demonstrating robustness and scalability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Latent Graph Sampling Generation (LGSG), a novel framework for generating semi-synthetic graphs of varying sizes. LGSG leverages diffusion models and node embeddings to overcome limitations of existing deep learning-based graph generation methods. Specifically, LGSG addresses the dependency on node IDs, enabling the generation of graphs larger than the input graph without retraining. The framework learns the distribution of node embeddings and subgraph structures, resulting in scalable and flexible graph generation. The paper evaluates LGSG against classic graph generative models, demonstrating comparable performance on standard metrics and superior performance on metrics related to community structure (clustering coefficient). The authors also perform a scalability study, showing that the framework maintains consistent structural characteristics across different graph sizes. They use two different approaches to assemble the generated graphs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper tackles a significant limitation in existing graph generation methods, namely the inability to generate graphs of arbitrary size and the reliance on node IDs. The use of diffusion models and node embeddings is a reasonable approach to address this limitation, and the authors present a clear and well-structured framework.

**Strengths:**

*   **Addressing a Key Limitation:** The paper directly confronts the scalability issue in deep learning-based graph generation, which is a significant hurdle for applying these techniques to large real-world networks.
*   **Node Embedding Approach:** Using node embeddings rather than node IDs allows the model to generalize and generate graphs beyond the size of the input.
*   **Scalability:**  The reported scalability study provides evidence that the proposed framework can handle varying graph sizes without significant performance degradation. The consistent structural characteristics are a plus.
*   **Community Structure Capture:** The better clustering coefficient performance suggests a meaningful advance in how the generator reflects graph organization.
*   **Relatively clear architecture description**: Makes the model relatively easy to understand and implement.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty in Core Components:** While the combination of diffusion models and node embeddings is novel in this *specific context*, both techniques are independently well-established in their respective fields. The novelty lies in the system-level integration and adaptation to the graph generation problem.  Also, node embedding is already well established for graph generation tasks.
*   **Baseline Choice:** The baselines are classic graph generative models, not the state-of-the-art. This is partially justified by the lack of scalability in SOTA deep generative models. However, the comparison feels somewhat unfair since the baselines were designed for different objectives and potentially different performance characteristics. A more modern baseline of classical graph models may have strengthened comparisons. The paper mentions GenCAT which is a more recent algorithm but does not detail how that algorithm compares to state of the art algorithms in that family. It is not obvious that GEN CAT does well with the classic metrics.
*   **Limited Real-world Application:** While the synthetic graphs demonstrate desirable properties, the paper lacks a concrete demonstration of how these generated graphs can benefit downstream tasks or applications. A real-world use case would strengthen the practical significance.
*   **Parameter Sensitivity:** The discussion of parameter sensitivity (threshold matching, number of nodes) highlights potential practical challenges. A more thorough analysis and guidelines for parameter selection would be beneficial.
*   **Metrics** Using metrics to capture properties from real world graphs, e.g degree distribution, does not necessarily mean that the reproduced metrics are realistic.
*   **Not a true graph generator** A true graph generator is trained on a dataset of small graphs and generates new graphs, unlike the algorithms in this study, that uses one graph as a base.
*   **No node attributes**: The graph generated in this study have no node attributes, but real graphs contain more information than edges and nodes.
*   **Computational Resources**: The paper does not mention the computational resources for training the model, so scalability is not necessarily improved.

**Significance:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by addressing the scalability limitations of deep learning-based graph generation. The ability to generate graphs of arbitrary size opens up possibilities for simulating and studying large-scale networks that are intractable with existing methods. The improvement in capturing community structure is also a positive sign.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper provides a practical contribution and moves the field forward. However, the limitations in baseline selection, the lack of a concrete application, and the parameter sensitivity concerns temper the assessment.  The combination of diffusion models and node embeddings is reasonably novel but not groundbreaking. The core building blocks are well-established. Furthermore, the use of classical models as baselines decreases the perceived novelty. While the results are promising, further work is needed to demonstrate their broader applicability and impact. For these reasons, the paper earns a score of 6.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.SI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Data Diversification Methods In Alignment Enhance Math Performance In LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02173v1)
- **Authors**: Berkan Dokmeci, Qingyang Wu, Ben Athiwaratkun, Ce Zhang, Shuaiwen Leon Song, James Zou
- **Abstract**: While recent advances in preference learning have enhanced alignment in human feedback, mathematical reasoning remains a persistent challenge. We investigate how data diversification strategies in preference optimization can improve the mathematical reasoning abilities of large language models (LLMs). We evaluate three common data generation methods: temperature sampling, Chain-of-Thought prompting, and Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS), and introduce Diversified-ThinkSolve (DTS), a novel structured approach that systematically decomposes problems into diverse reasoning paths. Our results show that with strategically diversified preference data, models can substantially improve mathematical reasoning performance, with the best approach yielding gains of 7.1% on GSM8K and 4.2% on MATH over the base model. Despite its strong performance, DTS incurs only a marginal computational overhead (1.03x) compared to the baseline, while MCTS is nearly five times more costly with lower returns. These findings demonstrate that structured exploration of diverse problem-solving methods creates more effective preference data for mathematical alignment than traditional approaches.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how data diversification strategies in preference optimization can improve the mathematical reasoning abilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). The authors evaluate three existing data generation methods (temperature sampling, Chain-of-Thought prompting, and Monte Carlo Tree Search) and introduce a novel approach called Diversified-ThinkSolve (DTS). DTS systematically decomposes problems into diverse reasoning paths before generating solutions. Results indicate that strategically diversified preference data substantially improves mathematical reasoning performance, with DTS yielding the best results with minimal computational overhead. The authors argue that structured exploration of diverse problem-solving methods creates more effective preference data for mathematical alignment than traditional approaches, and that data quality and diversity can be more crucial than simply optimizing algorithmic approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Novelty of DTS:** The Diversified-ThinkSolve (DTS) approach is a genuinely novel contribution. Decoupling reasoning path generation from solution execution is a smart way to inject diversity. The approach is well-motivated and tackles a known limitation of existing methods.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents a thorough evaluation across standard mathematics benchmarks (GSM8K and MATH) and several preference optimization methods (SFT, ORPO, DPO, SimPO). The inclusion of both average and best performance across epochs is helpful.
    * **Efficiency of DTS:**  The fact that DTS achieves significant performance gains with only a marginal computational overhead (1.03x) is a major selling point. This makes it a practical and attractive solution.
    * **Detailed Analysis:** The paper provides a detailed analysis of hyperparameter sensitivity, training dynamics, and the characteristics of solutions generated by different approaches. This adds depth and understanding.
    * **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly presents its methodology, results, and conclusions.  The illustrations are helpful.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Benchmark Limitations:** While GSM8K and MATH are standard benchmarks, they represent only a subset of mathematical reasoning tasks. Generalizability to other mathematical domains or real-world applications might be limited.
    * **Reward Model Dependency:** The reliance on a reward model, despite careful selection, introduces potential bias.  The paper acknowledges this, but further investigation into the impact of reward model bias could strengthen the work. The analysis of correct vs incorrect scoring by reward models is a good start in quantifying this potential bias.
    * **Model Scale:** Experiments were performed on a single (relatively small) model size (8B parameters). How these findings scale to larger models is an open question. While this is common limitation, it should be acknowledged.
    * **Dependency on external frameworks (DSPy and OptiLLM):** The experimental setup and results depend heavily on these external frameworks. While these frameworks are valuable tools, relying on their implementation details introduces a potential point of failure or incompatibility if the frameworks are updated or discontinued.
    * **Limited Zero-Shot Analysis:** the majority of the results focus on fine tuning data based on a "mixed correctness" filtering. Further emphasis should be given to zero-shot reasoning results.

* **Significance:**

    * **Advancing Preference Optimization:** The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of preference optimization for LLMs, specifically in the context of mathematical reasoning. It demonstrates that data diversification is a crucial factor for improving alignment.
    * **Practical Guidance:** The paper provides practical guidance for researchers and practitioners seeking to improve the mathematical reasoning abilities of LLMs through preference optimization. The hyperparameter sensitivity analysis is particularly valuable.

* **Potential Influence:**

    * The DTS approach could become a standard technique for generating preference data for mathematical alignment.
    * The findings regarding the importance of data diversity could influence future research in preference optimization for other domains.
    * The hyperparameter sensitivity analysis could guide the development of more robust and efficient preference optimization algorithms.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper introduces a novel and effective technique (DTS) for improving mathematical reasoning in LLMs through preference optimization. The evaluation is comprehensive, and the results are compelling. The practical benefits of DTS (low computational overhead) and the detailed analysis of hyperparameter sensitivity add significant value. While the paper has some limitations (benchmark scope, reward model dependency, reliance on external libraries), the strengths outweigh the weaknesses. The DTS algorithm is well motivated and implemented, creating a useful framework for further improvements to data diversification strategies. For these reasons, the paper justifies a high score.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### The Revolution Has Arrived: What the Current State of Large Language Models in Education Implies for the Future
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02180v1)
- **Authors**: Russell Beale
- **Abstract**: Large language Models have only been widely available since 2022 and yet in less than three years have had a significant impact on approaches to education and educational technology. Here we review the domains in which they have been used, and discuss a variety of use cases, their successes and failures. We then progress to discussing how this is changing the dynamic for learners and educators, consider the main design challenges facing LLMs if they are to become truly helpful and effective as educational systems, and reflect on the learning paradigms they support. We make clear that the new interaction paradigms they bring are significant and argue that this approach will become so ubiquitous it will become the default way in which we interact with technologies, and revolutionise what people expect from computer systems in general. This leads us to present some specific and significant considerations for the design of educational technology in the future that are likely to be needed to ensure acceptance by the changing expectations of learners and users.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've carefully reviewed the provided document. Here's a summary and critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper provides a comprehensive review of the current state and future implications of Large Language Models (LLMs) in education. It covers a wide range of topics, including intelligent tutoring, personalized learning support, content generation, instructional design, assessment, collaborative learning, and creative learning. The authors discuss how LLMs are changing learner interaction and educator roles, highlighting both the opportunities and challenges that these changes present. The paper also addresses key design considerations and sociotechnical concerns, such as accuracy, reliability, bias, fairness, academic integrity, ethical use, privacy, and explainability. Finally, the paper explores future research opportunities and offers insights into the impending revolution in technology design driven by LLMs.  The paper argues that these shifts will demand designers to adjust their approaches in order to better align with the changing expectations of learners and their overall acceptance of novel technological tools.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Scope:** The paper offers a broad and well-structured overview of the many facets of LLMs in education. It doesn't just focus on the positive aspects, but also acknowledges the potential pitfalls and challenges.
*   **Balanced Perspective:** The authors maintain a balanced viewpoint throughout, presenting both the excitement surrounding LLMs and the caution needed when implementing them.
*   **Forward-Looking Insights:** The paper goes beyond simply summarizing existing research and offers valuable insights into future trends and research opportunities. The discussions on the changing default interaction style and the impact on technology design are particularly insightful.
*   **Sociotechnical Considerations:** The paper gives due weight to the ethical and social implications of LLMs in education, addressing issues like bias, equity, and academic integrity.
*   **Well-Referenced:** The paper draws upon a wide array of recent research to support its claims, demonstrating a thorough understanding of the current state of the field.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Empirical Data (Future Focus):** While the paper reviews existing literature, a significant portion focuses on predicting future trends and user expectations. These are inherently speculative and, while well-reasoned, lack strong empirical validation. The authors acknowledge this by frequently mentioning "hypotheses" and "expectations"
*   **Depth vs. Breadth Trade-off:** Given the broad scope, the paper necessarily sacrifices some depth in certain areas. Some of the individual topics (e.g., AI literacy, explainability) could warrant dedicated papers on their own. This review instead opts for breadth, covering a wider swathe of related concerns.
*   **Limited Novelty:** The paper synthesizes existing research and perspectives rather than presenting fundamentally new ideas or experimental results. It doesn't introduce specific solutions or design frameworks, but rather highlights existing approaches and identifies areas for future exploration.
*   **Overly Optimistic Tone:** Despite acknowledging the risks, the paper still carries an overall tone of optimism about the potential of LLMs in education. A more critical and cautious stance in certain areas might be warranted, especially concerning the potential for widening existing inequalities.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's novelty lies primarily in its **synthesis** of a rapidly evolving field. It brings together research from diverse areas (computer science, education, HCI) to provide a cohesive picture of LLMs in education. The paper's significance lies in its ability to:

*   **Inform Stakeholders:** The paper serves as a valuable resource for educators, policymakers, and developers seeking to understand the opportunities and challenges of LLMs in education.
*   **Guide Future Research:** The identified research opportunities can help focus efforts in the field and address critical gaps in our knowledge.
*   **Shape Design Practices:** The insights into changing learner expectations can inform the design of future educational technologies, making them more effective and user-friendly.

**Justification for Score:**

Based on the strengths and weaknesses, I assign a score of **7**.

**Rationale:**

The paper is a valuable contribution to the field because it offers a comprehensive and balanced overview of LLMs in education. It is well-researched, insightful, and forward-looking. The main limitations are the lack of strong empirical evidence for some of the predictions and the somewhat optimistic tone. While the paper doesn't present radical new discoveries, it provides a valuable synthesis of existing knowledge and helps to chart a course for future research and development. The high score is therefore justifiable due to its wide-ranging impact and ability to inform stakeholders working in education-based development and assessment.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Enhancing COBOL Code Explanations: A Multi-Agents Approach Using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02182v1)
- **Authors**: Fangjian Lei, Jiawen Liu, Shayan Noei, Ying Zou, Derek Truong, William Alexander
- **Abstract**: Common Business Oriented Language (COBOL) is a programming language used to develop business applications that are widely adopted by financial, business, and government agencies. Due to its age, complexity, and declining number of COBOL developers, maintaining COBOL codebases is becoming increasingly challenging. In particular, the lack of documentation makes it difficult for new developers to effectively understand and maintain COBOL systems. Existing research utilizes large language models (LLMs) to explain the functionality of code snippets. However, COBOL presents unique challenges due to its architectural and syntactical differences, which often cause its code to exceed the token window size of LLMs. In this work, we propose a multi-agent approach that leverages two LLM-based agents working collaboratively to generate explanations for functions, files, and the overall project. These agents incorporate together by utilizing contextual information from the codebase into the code explanation prompts. We evaluate the effectiveness of our approach using 14 open-source, real-world COBOL projects. Our results indicate that our approach performs significantly better than the baseline in function code explanation, with improvements of 12.67%, 18.59%, and 0.62% in terms of METEOR, chrF, and SentenceBERT scores, respectively. At the file level, our approach effectively explains both short and long COBOL files that exceed the token window size of LLMs and surpass the baseline by 4.21%, 10.72%, and 14.68% in explaining the purpose, functionality, and clarity of the generated explanation. At the project level, our approach generates explanations that convey the functionality and purpose of 82% of the selected projects.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of understanding and maintaining legacy COBOL codebases, which are critical for many financial and government institutions but suffer from a lack of documentation and a declining pool of COBOL developers. The authors propose a multi-agent system leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate code explanations at the function, file, and project levels. This system uses two LLM-based agents: a Code Processing Agent (focused on understanding COBOL code) and a Text Processing Agent (focused on refining explanations).  The approach handles the token limit constraints of LLMs by hierarchically merging explanations of smaller code segments. The paper evaluates the approach on 14 open-source COBOL projects and demonstrates significant improvements over a baseline in terms of text similarity metrics and a LLM-as-a-judge evaluation. The paper emphasizes the practical benefits of automated COBOL documentation for new developers and legacy system maintenance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The approach shows a promising blend of modularization of complex code with LLMs for analysis. While prior work has explored LLMs for code explanation, this paper's novelty stems from its focus on COBOL, a language with specific architectural and syntactical challenges, its multi-agent architecture, and its ability to handle entire COBOL projects beyond individual code snippets or functions. The hierarchical approach to handling large files exceeding LLM token limits is another contribution. The use of a code-specific LLM (Granite) in conjunction with a general language model (GPT-4o) is also a notable aspect.
*   **Significance:** The work has significant practical implications. The decline in COBOL expertise and the continued reliance on legacy COBOL systems create a pressing need for automated tools that aid understanding and maintenance. A robust approach to automatically generating COBOL documentation can streamline the onboarding of new developers, reduce maintenance costs, and lower the risk of errors in critical systems. Moreover, the hierarchical technique to handling large files addresses a key bottleneck in applying LLMs to large codebases generally.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The experimental evaluation is comprehensive, including text similarity metrics, LLM-as-a-judge evaluation, and manual verification.
    *   The use of both a specialized code LLM and a general language LLM is well-justified and appears to be an effective strategy.
    *   The paper clearly defines the problem, the proposed solution, and the evaluation methodology.
    *   The replication package ensures the reproducibility of the results.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on README files for project-level explanations could be a limitation if the READMEs are outdated or incomplete. Though this is acknowledged and manually verified.
    *   While the paper addresses token limits, the computational cost of running the LLM agents (especially the Code Processing Agent) on large COBOL projects could be a factor for real-world deployment.
    *   The dominance of IBM projects in the dataset, while relevant, may limit the generalizability of the results to other COBOL environments or coding styles.
    *   The discussion of the ethical implications of automatically generating documentation is relatively limited. Potential biases in the LLMs and their impact on the generated explanations could be more thoroughly addressed.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in automated code documentation, particularly for legacy systems. The proposed multi-agent architecture and the hierarchical explanation generation technique could be adopted and adapted for other languages and codebases.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and practical approach to a significant problem in software engineering: maintaining legacy COBOL systems. The multi-agent architecture and the hierarchical explanation generation strategy are well-designed and effectively address the challenges of COBOL code and LLM token limits. The comprehensive evaluation provides strong evidence of the effectiveness of the approach. The paper would benefit from a more balanced dataset, a discussion of the computational costs, and a more comprehensive analysis of potential biases. However, the paper's strengths outweigh its weaknesses, and the potential impact on the field of software maintenance and legacy system management justifies a score of 7.5.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Computer Science Education in the Age of Generative AI
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02183v1)
- **Authors**: Russell Beale
- **Abstract**: Generative AI tools - most notably large language models (LLMs) like ChatGPT and Codex - are rapidly revolutionizing computer science education. These tools can generate, debug, and explain code, thereby transforming the landscape of programming instruction. This paper examines the profound opportunities that AI offers for enhancing computer science education in general, from coding assistance to fostering innovative pedagogical practices and streamlining assessments. At the same time, it highlights challenges including academic integrity concerns, the risk of over-reliance on AI, and difficulties in verifying originality. We discuss what computer science educators should teach in the AI era, how to best integrate these technologies into curricula, and the best practices for assessing student learning in an environment where AI can generate code, prototypes and user feedback. Finally, we propose a set of policy recommendations designed to harness the potential of generative AI while preserving the integrity and rigour of computer science education. Empirical data and emerging studies are used throughout to support our arguments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Computer Science Education in the Age of Generative AI" explores the opportunities and challenges presented by generative AI tools, particularly large language models (LLMs) like ChatGPT and Codex, in computer science (CS) education. It discusses how AI can enhance code generation, debugging, personalized learning, and collaborative learning.  It also highlights the risks associated with over-reliance on AI, academic dishonesty, and the potential for superficial understanding.  The paper proposes curricular adjustments to include AI literacy, ethical considerations, and modified pedagogical strategies. Finally, it recommends policy changes for CS departments to balance innovation with academic integrity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Overview:** The paper offers a broad and well-structured overview of the potential impacts of generative AI on CS education, considering various aspects from curriculum design to assessment strategies.
    *   **Balanced Perspective:** It acknowledges both the benefits and drawbacks of AI, avoiding an overly enthusiastic or alarmist tone. The paper engages with an emerging landscape of LLM capabilities, rather than a narrow set of assumptions about what it might be like in the future.
    *   **Practical Recommendations:**  The paper provides concrete suggestions for integrating AI into the classroom, redesigning assessments, and developing institutional policies. The paper suggests the use of modern tooling and workflows in the design process.
    *   **Up to Date:** The paper's reference list contains very recent studies, suggesting it is drawing upon a current landscape.
    *   **Engagement with Current Practices:** Rather than completely discarding the traditional curriculum the paper suggests a reframing of assessment rather than a full reset of all the course material.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Lack of Empirical Data:** While the paper references empirical studies, it doesn't present original empirical data or case studies to support its claims. The arguments are primarily based on observations and synthesized from existing literature. The paper relies on more general sources about GenAI instead of the more specific studies that are likely to emerge over the next couple years.
    *   **Limited Depth:** Given the breadth of topics covered, the paper lacks in-depth analysis of specific pedagogical strategies or assessment methods. The recommendations, while useful, remain relatively high-level.
    *   **Overly Optimistic Reliance on GenAI as Collabroator:** While the authors advocate for a measured approach, there's a tacit assumption that LLMs will inevitably improve and provide increasingly reliable assistance. This neglects the potential for LLMs to perpetuate biases, generate incorrect or misleading code/results, or be deployed to reinforce existing inequalities.
    *   **Focuses on Output:** The paper focuses significantly on what is being produced and its quality and effectiveness. There is little emphasis on the user experience, how to measure the quality of interaction and use these models.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    *   The paper's novelty lies primarily in its comprehensive synthesis of existing research and emerging trends. It doesn't introduce entirely new concepts or techniques but rather provides a valuable framework for thinking about the future of CS education in the age of AI.
    *   The significance of the paper stems from its timely and relevant discussion of a critical issue. As generative AI continues to evolve, CS educators will need to adapt their teaching practices, and this paper offers a useful starting point for that process. It frames the discussion well, highlighting the key considerations and challenges.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence discussions and policy-making within CS departments. It could serve as a valuable resource for faculty development workshops and curriculum redesign initiatives.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a useful synthesis of current thinking on a rapidly evolving topic. However, its lack of original empirical data and limited depth in specific areas prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution. The main strength is that it gathers together and synthesizes many other pieces of research and knowledge. Thus the score is a 7 out of 10.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 7/10

### EvalAssist: A Human-Centered Tool for LLM-as-a-Judge
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02186v1)
- **Authors**: Zahra Ashktorab, Elizabeth M. Daly, Erik Miehling, Werner Geyer, Martin Santillan Cooper, Tejaswini Pedapati, Michael Desmond, Qian Pan, Hyo Jin Do
- **Abstract**: With the broad availability of large language models and their ability to generate vast outputs using varied prompts and configurations, determining the best output for a given task requires an intensive evaluation process, one where machine learning practitioners must decide how to assess the outputs and then carefully carry out the evaluation. This process is both time-consuming and costly. As practitioners work with an increasing number of models, they must now evaluate outputs to determine which model and prompt performs best for a given task. LLMs are increasingly used as evaluators to filter training data, evaluate model performance, assess harms and risks, or assist human evaluators with detailed assessments. We present EvalAssist, a framework that simplifies the LLM-as-a-judge workflow. The system provides an online criteria development environment, where users can interactively build, test, and share custom evaluation criteria in a structured and portable format. We support a set of LLM-based evaluation pipelines that leverage off-the-shelf LLMs and use a prompt-chaining approach we developed and contributed to the UNITXT open-source library. Additionally, our system also includes specially trained evaluators to detect harms and risks in LLM outputs. We have deployed the system internally in our organization with several hundreds of users.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "EvalAssist: A Human-Centered Tool for LLM-as-a-Judge":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces EvalAssist, a framework designed to simplify and improve the process of using Large Language Models (LLMs) as judges for evaluating other LLM outputs.  It provides a user-friendly online environment where users can interactively create, test, and share custom evaluation criteria in a structured and portable format. EvalAssist leverages off-the-shelf LLMs and a prompt-chaining approach contributed to the UNITXT open-source library. The system also includes specialized trained evaluators for detecting harms and risks.  It supports both direct assessment (rubric-based) and pairwise comparison evaluation methods, incorporates positional bias indicators, and offers an SDK for scaling evaluations to larger datasets. The paper highlights design goals addressing challenges faced by engineers and researchers during model evaluation and reports on internal deployment and user evaluation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has moderate novelty. While the concept of using LLMs as judges is not entirely new, EvalAssist offers a comprehensive framework integrating several useful features:

    *   *Combined Features:* Integrating criteria development, testing, bias indicators (positional bias), multiple LLM evaluator choices, and scalable evaluation into a single tool provides a more complete solution than many existing tools.
    *   *UNITXT Integration:* Leveraging and extending the UNITXT open-source library is a plus, promoting reusability and community contribution.
    *   *Harms and Risks Evaluators:* The inclusion of specially trained evaluators for harms and risks adds practical value.
    *   *Support for both Direct Assessment and Pairwise comparison.* This offers flexibilty to the user to choose the most appropriate LLM-as-a-Judge approach for their usecase.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper lies in its potential to improve the efficiency, transparency, and reliability of LLM evaluation. By providing a structured and accessible environment for criteria development and evaluation, EvalAssist can empower users to:

    *   *Reduce Evaluation Costs:* The tool helps reduce time and cost of human evaluations.
    *   *Increase Transparency:* The inclusion of bias indicators helps users to understand and mitigate potential biases in the LLM judgments.
    *   *Improve Alignment:* The framework aligns AI-assisted evaluations with diverse stakeholder needs.
    *   *Scale Evaluations:* The provision for scaling to larger datasets allows for thorough model validation.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   *Human-Centered Design:* The tool is designed based on user needs identified through prior work and user studies.
    *   *Practical Features:* EvalAssist provides key feature, such as bias indicators, that are very useful when using LLM-as-a-Judges.
    *   *Open-Source Friendly:* EvalAssist is built on top of an open source library which allows easy collaboration in the field.
    *   *Deployed User Base:* Having a deployed user base within the organization provides empirical evidence for its utility.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   *Limited Scope:* The paper describes an internal deployment, but provides limited detailed quantitative evaluation of the tool's performance (e.g., speed of evaluation, comparative accuracy to human evaluation). More detailed evaluations in future versions of this work are needed.
    *   *Lack of Detailed Bias Mitigation:* The paper mentions positional bias, but doesn't describe more comprehensive bias mitigation strategies beyond detecting it. More elaborate bias analysis and tools to mitigate bias are required for adoption in enterprise setting.
    *   *Dependency on LLM quality:* The quality of the evaluation relies heavily on the underlying LLMs. The paper acknowledges this, but further discussion of how to select, fine-tune, or ensemble LLMs for optimal evaluation would be beneficial.
